Đề trial
I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints.  The system must

Đề ôn tập
PRACTICE TEST 1: E-COMMERCE & SMART LOGISTICS
I. CASE STUDY
"SwiftMart" is a rapidly growing online grocery platform currently struggling with a manual
backend that leads to frequent delivery delays. The company has commissioned an "Integrated
Fulfillment System" (IFS) to digitize its operations. Customers use the web and mobile platform
to browse products, manage their shopping carts, and process payments. A unique feature is
the "Subscription Mode," where customers can schedule recurring weekly deliveries; the system
automatically generates these orders every Monday. Delivery Riders use a dedicated mobile
interface to accept delivery assignments, navigate to customer locations using integrated maps,
and update the order status to "Delivered." Warehouse Staff are responsible for inventory
management, including updating stock levels and marking items as "Damaged" or "Out of
Stock." When inventory for a high-demand item falls below a certain threshold, the system
automatically triggers a "Restock Alert" to the Procurement Team. Managers oversee the
platform by viewing real-time sales analytics, managing promotional discount codes, and
Technically, the IFS must integrate with a third-party Payment Gateway and a Mapping
Service API. The system must handle up to 5,000 transactions per hour with a sub-2-second

Tennicians monitor the status or cardio machines; the machines are equipped with sensors  7. 1.
that automatically send "Error Reports" to the SMS if a mechanical failure is detected.
The SMS must ensure that all staff actions are governed by a Role-Based Access Control
(RBAC) system. To ensure safety, the system must limit the number of active QR code entries to
the gym's maximum capacity (150 people). The mobile app must be accessible for visually
impaired users (WCAG 2.1 compliance). Data backups must occur every 24 hours to an off-site
cloud server to prevent data loss.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)

they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the SMS. For each use case, state the initiating
actors) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)

I. CASE STUDY
"SkyPath International Airport" requires a new "Passenger Service System" (PSS) to handle the
surge in holiday travelers. Passengers use the PSS kiosks or mobile app to check-in for flights,
select seats, and generate digital boarding passes. During check-in, passengers may optionally

course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff  1 of 6
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (BAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For Each actor, state their primary
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating
actors) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)
3. Draw a complete UML Use Case Diagram for the CRS. The diagram must include: (0.8
points)
• All identified actors, placed correctly inside or outside the system boundary
• All major use cases inside the system boundary rectangle

Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating
actor(s) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)
3. Draw a complete UML Use Case Diagram for the CRS. The diagram must include: (0.8
• All identified actors, placed correctly inside or outside the system boundary
• All major use cases inside the system boundary rectangle
• At least ONE <<include>> relationship - provide a brief written rationale
• At least ONE <<extend>> relationship - provide a brief written rationale
• Any actor-generalisation relationships that apply

ASU USE-CASE MODEL: ACTOR DESCRIPTIONS
A person who is responsible for maintaining the curriculum
information inclusive of students, professors and courses.
Registrar  The Registrar uses the Course Registration System to
Discovered from  prepare the course catalogue for the coming term and to
domain experts.  manage course offerings.
A person who is registered to take courses at the university.
A student uses the Course Registration System to register
Student  for courses in the current or a future term.
An external system responsible for billing students. After a
student successfully registers for a term, the Course
Billing System  Registration System sends the billing information to the
billing system.

=  The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating
actors) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)
3. Draw a complete UML Use Case Diagram for the CRS. The diagram must include: (0.8
points)
• All identified actors, placed correctly inside or outside the system boundary
• All major use cases inside the system boundary rectangle
• At least ONE <<include>> relationship - provide a brief written rationale
• At least ONE <<extend>> relationship - provide a brief written rationale
• Any actor-generalisation relationships that apply

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints.  The system must

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades,  and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time

Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades,  and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.

Normal text -  Arial  | - 11] +
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
ENG  3:40 PM
VI  4/18/2026

5A8100%  Normal text -  Arial  - 11) +  Editing
sorr amoron main viewe
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
ENG  3:40 PM
VI  4/18/2026

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades,  and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both deskton and mobile browsers and must conform to

catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
ENG  3:43 PM
US  4/18/2026

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades,  and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both deskton and mobile browsers and must conform to

The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades,  and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both deskton and mobile browsers and must conform to

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades,  and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both deskton and mobile browsers and must conform to

staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to

The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages

remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
E  ENG  3:48 PM
US  4/18/2026

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to

with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time  1 of 7
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating
actors) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)
3. Draw a complete UML Use Case Diagram for the CRS. The diagram must include: (0.8
points)
• All identified actors, placed correctly inside or outside the system boundary
• All major use cases inside the system boundary rectangle

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registrationirequests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
ENG  3:52 PM
US  4/18/2026

Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary  1of7
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating
actors) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)

• All identified actors, placed correctly inside or outside the system boundary

Normal text  Arial  EX  Editing
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary

scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.

### 35:55
cốc cốc  Luện tập Q2 - Google Doc  +  La

File Edit View Insert Format Tools  Extensions Help
Normal text  Arial  - 11+ BI UA•  Editing
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary
man or cecondar actor anointe

Normal text  Arial  - 11+
.. Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academ.. L..
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their  1 of 7
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
hittire veare

goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)

slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workfiow.  1 of 7
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the reyistration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.

I. CASE STUDY
Green University is a mid-sized institution with approximately 8,000 students and 400 academic
staff. Each semester, the university must manage course enrollment for all undergraduate and
postgraduate programmes. Currently, students submit paper-based registration forms to their
faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
N-
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and

registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating

automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.

course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.  •
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsivehandling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in
future years.
Question 2: UC Modeling (1.5 points)
1. Identify ALL actors in the Course Registration System. For each actor, state their primary
goal and whether they are a primary or secondary actor. (0.3 points)
2. List at least EIGHT use cases for the CRS. For each use case, state the initiating
co  actors) and a one-sentence description of the goal. (0.4 points)
3. Draw a complete UML Use Case Diagram for the CRS. The diagram must include: (0.8
points)
All identified actors, placed correctly inside or outside the system boundary
• All major use cases inside the system boundary rectangle

faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the
catalogue of available courses, check their eligibility based on completed prerequisites, submit
registration requests, and track the status of each request in real time. If a course is fully
booked, a student may join an electronic waitlist; should a registered student later drop the
course, the system automatically notifies and enrolls the first eligible student on the waitlist.
Lecturers need to review the roster of students enrolled in their courses, record midterm and
final grades, and release grades to students once the grading period closes. Academic Staff
(administrators) are responsible for defining the semester timetable, setting and publishing
course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are
sensitive, all communication must be encrypted using TLS 1.2 or higher, and access must be
governed by a role-based access control (RBAC) model so that each user sees only the
functions and data relevant to their role. The platform must be available 99.5% of the time
during the semester, with any planned maintenance scheduled outside the registration window.
The interface must be accessible on both desktop and mobile browsers and must conform to
WCAG 2.1 Level AA accessibility standards. The architecture should also support horizontal
scaling so that additional server capacity can be added smoothly as student numbers grow in

faculty office, and clerks manually enter the data into spreadsheets. This process is error-prone,
slow, and creates long queues during the registration window. The university's administration
has therefore decided to commission a web-based Course Registration System (CRS) to
automate and streamline the entire workflow.
The CRS is expected to serve four main groups of users. Students need to browse the

course quotas, opening and closing the registration window, approving exceptional cases, and
generating enrolment and grade-distribution reports. Finally, a System Administrator manages
user accounts, configures system parameters, and oversees scheduled data backups.
From a technical standpoint, the university imposes several constraints. The system must
remain responsive-handling at least 1,000 simultaneous users during peak registration periods
with a page-response time under 3 seconds. Because student records and grade data are

3. Draw a complete UM USe Case Diagram tor the PCMS. The diagram must incude:
(0.8 points)
• All identified actors and major use cases.
• At least ONE <<include>> and ONE <<extend>> relationship with rationales.
• Any actor-generalisation relationships that apply.
PRACTICE TEST 3: SMART GYM & FACILITY
MANAGEMENT
I. CASE STUDY
"IronTemple Gym" is transitioning to a "Smart Facility Management System" (SMS) to handle
its 3,000 members. Members access the gym by scanning a QR code generated by the app,
book slots for group classes (like Yoga or HIIT), and track their personal workout logs. If a
member reaches a 30-day "Attendance Streak," the system generates a "Free Protein Shake"
voucher. Personal Trainers manage their client rosters, design custom workout plans, and
schedule 1-on-1 coaching sessions. If a session is booked, the system sends an automated
reminder via an external SMS Gateway. Front Desk Staff handle new membership  6 of 8
